This skill covers extracting text content, managing text layout, and accessing PDF metadata.\n\n## Libraries and Installation\n\n### PyPDF2 (Basic PDF operations)\n```bash\npip install PyPDF2\n```\n- Good for: Reading basic text, accessing metadata, splitting pages\n- Limitations: Text extraction quality varies, doesn't preserve layout well\n\n### pdfplumber (High-quality text extraction)\n```bash\npip install pdfplumber\n```\n- Good for: Precise text extraction, table detection, position information\n- Best for: Understanding document structure and position-based redaction\n- Preserves text positioning for accurate replacement\n\n### pypdf (Fork of PyPDF2)\n```bash\npip install pypdf\n```\n- Modern successor to PyPDF2\n- Better text handling and PDF manipulation\n\n## Common Patterns\n\n### Extract all text from PDF\n```python\nimport pdfplumber\n\nwith pdfplumber.open(\"document.pdf\") as pdf:\n    for page in pdf.pages:\n        text = page.extract_text()\n        print(text)\n```\n\n### Get text with positioning\n```python\nwith pdfplumber.open(\"document.pdf\") as pdf:\n    page = pdf.pages[0]\n    words = page.extract_words()\n    # words contains: text, x0, top, x1, bottom, size, etc.\n```\n\n### Access PDF metadata\n```python\nimport PyPDF2\n\nreader = PyPDF2.PdfReader(\"document.pdf\")\nmetadata = reader.metadata\nprint(metadata)  # Author, Creator, Subject, etc.\n```\n\n## Key Challenges\n\n1. **Text Extraction Quality**: PDFs may have complex layouts, columns, or scanned images\n2. **Character Encoding**: Some PDFs have encoding issues\n3. **Position Preservation**: Text position info is needed for redaction\n4. **Metadata Removal**: Must clear all embedded author/creator info\n\n## Best Practices\n\n- Use pdfplumber for detailed text analysis and positioning\n- Use pypdf for writing/modifying PDFs\n- Always check both visible text and metadata for sensitive info\n- Test extraction on sample pages before full processing\n"}}],"stop_reason":null,"stop_sequence":null,"usage":{"input_tokens":5,"cache_creation_input_tokens":342,"cache_read_input_tokens":19064,"cache_creation":{"ephemeral_5m_input_tokens":342,"ephemeral_1h_input_tokens":0},"output_tokens":1},"context_management":null},"parent_tool_use_id":null,"session_id":"4d4cb9a5-eb57-4c9d-870e-459a68c3f925","uuid":"d94f3606-3c7d-45b1-8e49-ee5da55ca7b1"}

This skill covers various redaction approaches for anonymizing documents.\n\n## Libraries and Installation\n\n### ReportLab (Drawing and redaction)\n```bash\npip install reportlab\n```\n- Good for: Creating overlays to cover text\n- Can draw rectangles over sensitive areas\n\n### pypdf / PyPDF2 (PDF modification)\nAlready covered in extraction skill, but also used for modification\n\n### pikepdf (Advanced PDF manipulation)\n```bash\npip install pikepdf\n```\n- Good for: Direct PDF stream manipulation\n- Can modify PDF content streams directly\n\n## Redaction Approaches\n\n### 1. Overlay Method (Most Compatible)\nCreate a new PDF with black boxes over sensitive text:\n```python\nfrom reportlab.pdfgen import canvas\nfrom reportlab.lib.pagesizes import letter\nfrom PyPDF2 import PdfReader, PdfWriter\nfrom io import BytesIO\n\n# Create overlay with black boxes at specific positions\nbuffer = BytesIO()\nc = canvas.Canvas(buffer, pagesize=letter)\n# c.rect(x0, y0, width, height, fill=1, stroke=0)  # Black filled rectangle\nc.save()\n\n# Merge overlay with original PDF\n```\n\n### 2. Stream Content Modification\nRemove/modify text directly in PDF streams:\n```python\nimport pikepdf\n\npdf = pikepdf.open(\"document.pdf\")\n# Modify content streams to remove text objects\npdf.save(\"redacted.pdf\")\n```\n\n### 3. Character Replacement\nReplace visible text while keeping PDF structure:\n- Extract text, replace sensitive content, re-embed text\n- Most complex, requires text positioning precision\n\n## Anonymization Targets\n\n### Author Information\n- PDF metadata (Author, Creator, Producer fields)\n- Author names in document text\n- Affiliation information\n- Email addresses\n\n### Venue/Publishing Information\n- Conference/journal names in text\n- Acceptance notices\n- Submission IDs\n\n### Identifiers\n- arXiv IDs (format: YYMM.NNNNN or archive/NNNNNNN)\n- DOI numbers\n- URLs with identifying information\n\n### Self-Citations\n- Usually acceptable if all other author info is redacted\n- But can be redacted if author name would be revealed\n\n## Implementation Strategy\n\n1. **Extract text** from all pages to find sensitive content\n2. **Identify locations** of sensitive text (coordinates, page numbers)\n3. **Remove metadata** (Author, Creator, etc.)\n4. **Create redaction overlays** with black boxes\n5. **Merge overlays** with original PDF\n6. **Verify redaction** - check that no info leaks\n\n## Common Patterns for Detection\n\n```python\nimport re\n\n# arXiv ID patterns\narxiv_pattern = r'\\b(\\d{4}\\.\\d{4,5}|[a-z\\-]+/\\d{7})\\b'\n\n# Email patterns\nemail_pattern = r'\\b[A-Za-z0-9._%+-]+@[A-Za-z0-9.-]+\\.[A-Z|a-z]{2,}\\b'\n\n# Common venue names\nvenues = ['NeurIPS', 'ICML', 'ICCV', 'AAAI', 'IJCAI', 'ECCV', 'CVPR']\n\n# Affiliation patterns\naffiliation_indicators = ['University', 'Institute', 'Department', 'Laboratory']\n```\n\n## Quality Assurance\n\n- Visually inspect redacted PDFs\n- Check that redactions are completely black (no text showing through)\n- Verify metadata is cleared\n- Test PDF is still readable/viewable\n"}}],"stop_reason":null,"stop_sequence":null,"usage":{"input_tokens":5,"cache_creation_input_tokens":342,"cache_read_input_tokens":19064,"cache_creation":{"ephemeral_5m_input_tokens":342,"ephemeral_1h_input_tokens":0},"output_tokens":1},"context_management":null},"parent_tool_use_id":null,"session_id":"4d4cb9a5-eb57-4c9d-870e-459a68c3f925","uuid":"589f2471-66c3-4577-b72b-5ce5144a4ab1"}
{"type":"assistant","message":{"model":"claude-haiku-4-5-20251001","id":"msg_vrtx_01MxCRBaTA4PMFNBjS1KzLMw","type":"message","role":"assistant","content":[{"type":"tool_use","id":"toolu_vrtx_01LX6BYqune8MvsUZL53QYKs","name":"Write","input":{"file_path":"/root/environment/skills/document-anonymization-patterns.md","content":"# Document Anonymization Patterns\n\n## Overview\nCommon patterns and strategies for identifying and redacting sensitive information in academic documents. This skill provides detection patterns and sanitization strategies.\n\n## Author Identity Leakage Vectors\n\n### 1. Direct Author Information\n- **Names in text**: \"John Smith et al.\", \"Smith (2023)\"\n- **Affiliations**: \"MIT\", \"Stanford University Department of...\", \"Prof. Jane Doe\"\n- **Contact info**: Email addresses, phone numbers\n- **ORCID IDs**: Persistent author identifiers\n- **Biography sections**: Author bios at paper end\n\n### 2. Metadata Fields\n- **PDF Author field**: Set by document creator\n- **Creator field**: Application that created PDF\n- **Producer field**: PDF software used\n- **Creation/modification dates**: May indicate author patterns\n- **Keywords/Subject**: May contain author names\n\n### 3. Venue Information\n- **Accepted at**: \"Accepted to NeurIPS 2024\"\n- **Submission ID**: \"Paper #12345 submitted to ICML\"\n- **Proceedings**: \"Proceedings of CVPR 2023\"\n- **DOI**: May identify paper and authors\n- **Conference/Journal headers**: Identifies publication venue\n\n### 4. Identifier Leakage\n- **arXiv IDs**: Direct link to paper metadata (author, submission date)\n  - Format examples: \"2401.12345\" or \"cs.CV/0512006\"\n  - arXiv metadata reveals authors\n- **Supplementary Material Links**: URLs to author-controlled sites\n- **Author's Website**: In references or contact sections\n- **GitHub/Code Links**: Often contain author identities\n\n### 5. Subtle Clues\n- **Self-citations**: If author name redacted elsewhere, self-cites still reveal author\n- **Unique writing style**: Can identify author (harder to address)\n- **Previous work references**: \"As shown in Smith et al. (2022)\" when Smith is an author\n- **Acknowledgments**: \"Thanks to funding from NSF Grant XYZ\"\n- **Timing**: Publication date + conference dates narrow identity\n\n## Detection Patterns\n\n### Regex Patterns for Common Identifiers\n\n```python\nimport re\n\npatterns = {\n    'arxiv': r'\\b(?:arXiv:)?(\\d{4}\\.\\d{4,5}|[a-z\\-]+(?:\\.[A-Z]{2})?/\\d{7}(?:v\\d+)?)\\b',\n    'doi': r'\\b(?:doi\\.org/|DOI:)[\\s]?(10\\.\\S+)',\n    'email': r'\\b[A-Za-z0-9._%+-]+@[A-Za-z0-9.-]+\\.[A-Z|a-z]{2,}\\b',\n    'url': r'https?://(?:www\\.)?([a-zA-Z0-9-]+\\.)+[a-zA-Z]{2,}(?:/\\S*)?',\n    'orcid': r'(?:\\d{4}-){3}\\d{3}[0-9X]',\n    'institution': r'\\b(?:University|Institute|Department|Laboratory|College|School|Center|Lab)\\b',\n}\n\n# Name pattern (very broad - requires context)\nname_pattern = r'\\b(?:Prof\\.|Dr\\.|Mr\\.|Ms\\.|Mrs\\.)?[A-Z][a-z]+(?:\\s+(?:and\\s+)?[A-Z][a-z]+)*\\b'\n```\n\n### Venue Name Patterns\nCommon conference/journal abbreviations:\n- NeurIPS, ICML, ICCV, AAAI, IJCAI, ECCV, CVPR, ICLR\n- JMLR, TPAMI, IEEE Transactions\n- SIGMOD, VLDB, ICDE, KDD\n\n## Redaction Strategy\n\n### Conservative Approach (Maximum Safety)\n- Redact all author names, affiliations, identifiers\n- Remove all venue/publication information\n- Remove all URLs and contact information\n- Clear all PDF metadata\n\n### Balanced Approach (Practical)\n- Redact author names and affiliations\n- Redact arXiv IDs and DOI numbers\n- Redact venue/publication information\n- Keep self-citations (since author info removed)\n- Clear PDF metadata\n\n### Minimal Approach (Preserving Content)\n- Only redact explicit author names and institutions\n- Keep arxiv ID (it's on arXiv anyway)\n- Keep venue information (public knowledge)\n- Still clear PDF metadata\n\n## Implementation Checklist\n\n- [ ] Extract all text from all pages\n- [ ] Identify all sensitive content (names, emails, URLs, IDs)\n- [ ] Remove PDF metadata (Author, Creator, etc.)\n- [ ] Create visual redaction overlays (black boxes)\n- [ ] Merge overlays with original PDF\n- [ ] Verify no text shows through redactions\n- [ ] Check that PDF remains readable\n- [ ] Test document in multiple PDF readers\n- [ ] Manual review of sensitive areas\n"}}],"stop_reason":null,"stop_sequence":null,"usage":{"input_tokens":5,"cache_creation_input_tokens":342,"cache_read_input_tokens":19064,"cache_creation":{"ephemeral_5m_input_tokens":342,"ephemeral_1h_input_tokens":0},"output_tokens":1},"context_management":null},"parent_tool_use_id":null,"session_id":"4d4cb9a5-eb57-4c9d-870e-459a68c3f925","uuid":"c34872c6-fe75-4050-8150-140864140dbe"}

Character Replacement\nReplace visible text while keeping PDF structure:\n- Extract text, replace sensitive content, re-embed text\n- Most complex, requires text positioning precision\n\n## Anonymization Targets\n\n### Author Information\n- PDF metadata (Author, Creator, Producer fields)\n- Author names in document text\n- Affiliation information\n- Email addresses\n\n### Venue/Publishing Information\n- Conference/journal names in text\n- Acceptance notices\n- Submission IDs\n\n### Identifiers\n- arXiv IDs (format: YYMM.NNNNN or archive/NNNNNNN)\n- DOI numbers\n- URLs with identifying information\n\n### Self-Citations\n- Usually acceptable if all other author info is redacted\n- But can be redacted if author name would be revealed\n\n## Implementation Strategy\n\n1. **Extract text** from all pages to find sensitive content\n2. **Identify locations** of sensitive text (coordinates, page numbers)\n3. **Remove metadata** (Author, Creator, etc.)\n4. **Create redaction overlays** with black boxes\n5. **Merge overlays** with original PDF\n6. **Verify redaction** - check that no info leaks\n\n## Common Patterns for Detection\n\n```python\nimport re\n\n# arXiv ID patterns\narxiv_pattern = r'\\b(\\d{4}\\.\\d{4,5}|[a-z\\-]+/\\d{7})\\b'\n\n# Email patterns\nemail_pattern = r'\\b[A-Za-z0-9._%+-]+@[A-Za-z0-9.-]+\\.[A-Z|a-z]{2,}\\b'\n\n# Common venue names\nvenues = ['NeurIPS', 'ICML', 'ICCV', 'AAAI', 'IJCAI', 'ECCV', 'CVPR']\n\n# Affiliation patterns\naffiliation_indicators = ['University', 'Institute', 'Department', 'Laboratory']\n```\n\n## Quality Assurance\n\n- Visually inspect redacted PDFs\n- Check that redactions are completely black (no text showing through)\n- Verify metadata is cleared\n- Test PDF is still readable/viewable\n","structuredPatch":[],"originalFile":null}}

This skill provides detection patterns and sanitization strategies.\n\n## Author Identity Leakage Vectors\n\n### 1. Direct Author Information\n- **Names in text**: \"John Smith et al.\", \"Smith (2023)\"\n- **Affiliations**: \"MIT\", \"Stanford University Department of...\", \"Prof. Jane Doe\"\n- **Contact info**: Email addresses, phone numbers\n- **ORCID IDs**: Persistent author identifiers\n- **Biography sections**: Author bios at paper end\n\n### 2. Metadata Fields\n- **PDF Author field**: Set by document creator\n- **Creator field**: Application that created PDF\n- **Producer field**: PDF software used\n- **Creation/modification dates**: May indicate author patterns\n- **Keywords/Subject**: May contain author names\n\n### 3. Venue Information\n- **Accepted at**: \"Accepted to NeurIPS 2024\"\n- **Submission ID**: \"Paper #12345 submitted to ICML\"\n- **Proceedings**: \"Proceedings of CVPR 2023\"\n- **DOI**: May identify paper and authors\n- **Conference/Journal headers**: Identifies publication venue\n\n### 4. Identifier Leakage\n- **arXiv IDs**: Direct link to paper metadata (author, submission date)\n  - Format examples: \"2401.12345\" or \"cs.CV/0512006\"\n  - arXiv metadata reveals authors\n- **Supplementary Material Links**: URLs to author-controlled sites\n- **Author's Website**: In references or contact sections\n- **GitHub/Code Links**: Often contain author identities\n\n### 5. Subtle Clues\n- **Self-citations**: If author name redacted elsewhere, self-cites still reveal author\n- **Unique writing style**: Can identify author (harder to address)\n- **Previous work references**: \"As shown in Smith et al. (2022)\" when Smith is an author\n- **Acknowledgments**: \"Thanks to funding from NSF Grant XYZ\"\n- **Timing**: Publication date + conference dates narrow identity\n\n## Detection Patterns\n\n### Regex Patterns for Common Identifiers\n\n```python\nimport re\n\npatterns = {\n    'arxiv': r'\\b(?:arXiv:)?(\\d{4}\\.\\d{4,5}|[a-z\\-]+(?:\\.[A-Z]{2})?/\\d{7}(?:v\\d+)?)\\b',\n    'doi': r'\\b(?:doi\\.org/|DOI:)[\\s]?(10\\.\\S+)',\n    'email': r'\\b[A-Za-z0-9._%+-]+@[A-Za-z0-9.-]+\\.[A-Z|a-z]{2,}\\b',\n    'url': r'https?://(?:www\\.)?([a-zA-Z0-9-]+\\.)+[a-zA-Z]{2,}(?:/\\S*)?',\n    'orcid': r'(?:\\d{4}-){3}\\d{3}[0-9X]',\n    'institution': r'\\b(?:University|Institute|Department|Laboratory|College|School|Center|Lab)\\b',\n}\n\n# Name pattern (very broad - requires context)\nname_pattern = r'\\b(?:Prof\\.|Dr\\.|Mr\\.|Ms\\.|Mrs\\.)?[A-Z][a-z]+(?:\\s+(?:and\\s+)?[A-Z][a-z]+)*\\b'\n```\n\n### Venue Name Patterns\nCommon conference/journal abbreviations:\n- NeurIPS, ICML, ICCV, AAAI, IJCAI, ECCV, CVPR, ICLR\n- JMLR, TPAMI, IEEE Transactions\n- SIGMOD, VLDB, ICDE, KDD\n\n## Redaction Strategy\n\n### Conservative Approach (Maximum Safety)\n- Redact all author names, affiliations, identifiers\n- Remove all venue/publication information\n- Remove all URLs and contact information\n- Clear all PDF metadata\n\n### Balanced Approach (Practical)\n- Redact author names and affiliations\n- Redact arXiv IDs and DOI numbers\n- Redact venue/publication information\n- Keep self-citations (since author info removed)\n- Clear PDF metadata\n\n### Minimal Approach (Preserving Content)\n- Only redact explicit author names and institutions\n- Keep arxiv ID (it's on arXiv anyway)\n- Keep venue information (public knowledge)\n- Still clear PDF metadata\n\n## Implementation Checklist\n\n- [ ] Extract all text from all pages\n- [ ] Identify all sensitive content (names, emails, URLs, IDs)\n- [ ] Remove PDF metadata (Author, Creator, etc.)\n- [ ] Create visual redaction overlays (black boxes)\n- [ ] Merge overlays with original PDF\n- [ ] Verify no text shows through redactions\n- [ ] Check that PDF remains readable\n- [ ] Test document in multiple PDF readers\n- [ ] Manual review of sensitive areas\n","structuredPatch":[],"originalFile":null}}

Find words that contain this match\n                    char_count = 0\n                    match_words = []\n                    for word in words:\n                        word_start = char_count\n                        word_end = char_count + len(word['text'])\n\n                        # Add padding\n                        padding = 1\n                        rects.append({\n                            'page': page_num,\n                            'x0': x0 - padding,\n                            'top': top - padding,\n                            'x1': x1 + padding,\n                            'bottom': bottom + padding\n                        })\n\n        return rects\n\n    def create_redaction_overlay(self, pdf_path, rects):\n        \"\"\"Create a PDF overlay with black rectangles covering sensitive content.\"\"\"\n        reader = PdfReader(pdf_path)\n        writer = PdfWriter()\n\n        # Group rectangles by page\n        rects_by_page = {}\n        for rect in rects:\n            page = rect['page']\n            if page not in rects_by_page:\n                rects_by_page[page] = []\n            rects_by_page[page].append(rect)\n\n        # Process each page\n        for page_num, page_obj in enumerate(reader.pages):\n            writer.add_page(page_obj)\n\n            # Create overlay for this page if needed\n            if page_num in rects_by_page:\n                page_rects = rects_by_page[page_num]\n\n                # Create overlay canvas\n                overlay_buffer = BytesIO()\n                overlay_canvas = canvas.Canvas(overlay_buffer, pagesize=(\n                    float(page_obj.mediabox.width),\n                    float(page_obj.mediabox.height)\n                ))\n\n                # Draw black rectangles\n                for rect in page_rects:\n                    x0, top, x1, bottom = rect['x0'], rect['top'], rect['x1'], rect['bottom']\n                    height = float(page_obj.mediabox.height)\n\n                    # Convert PDF coordinates (top-left origin) to reportlab (bottom-left)\n                    y0 = height - bottom\n                    y1 = height - top\n\n                    overlay_canvas.setFillColor((0, 0, 0))\n                    overlay_canvas.rect(x0, y0, x1 - x0, y1 - y0, fill=1, stroke=0)\n\n                overlay_canvas.save()\n                overlay_buffer.seek(0)\n\n                # Merge overlay with page\n                from PyPDF2 import PdfReader as PDFReader\n                overlay_pdf = PDFReader(overlay_buffer)\n                overlay_page = overlay_pdf.pages[0]\n                writer.pages[-1].merge_page(overlay_page)\n\n        return writer\n\n    def clear_metadata(self, writer):\n        \"\"\"Remove sensitive metadata from PDF.\"\"\"\n        if writer.metadata is None:\n            writer.add_metadata({})\n\n        # Clear potentially sensitive metadata fields\n        sensitive_fields = ['/Author', '/Creator', '/Producer', '/Subject', '/Keywords']\n        for field in sensitive_fields:\n            if field in writer.metadata:\n                del writer.metadata[field]\n\n        # Set minimal metadata\n        writer.add_metadata({\n            '/Producer': 'Anonymized PDF',\n        })\n\n    def anonymize_pdf(self, input_path, output_path):\n        \"\"\"Main anonymization pipeline.\"\"\"\n        print(f\"\\nProcessing: {input_path}\")\n        print(\"=\" * 60)\n\n        # Step 1: Extract text and find sensitive content\n        print(\"1. Extracting text and finding sensitive content...\")\n        text_data = self.extract_text_with_positions(input_path)\n\n        # Collect all text for pattern matching\n        full_text = \"\\n\".join([page['text'] or \"\" for page in text_data.values()])\n\n        # Find patterns\n        sensitive_patterns = {}\n        for pattern_name, pattern in self.patterns.items():\n            matches = list(re.finditer(pattern, full_text, re.IGNORECASE))\n            if matches:\n                print(f\"   Found {len(matches)} {pattern_name} identifiers\")\n                sensitive_patterns[pattern_name] = pattern\n\n        # Step 2: Find redaction rectangles\n        print(\"2. Finding text regions to redact...\")\n        rects = self.find_redaction_rects(text_data, sensitive_patterns)\n        print(f\"   Found {len(rects)} regions to redact\")\n\n        # Step 3: Create overlay and merge\n        print(\"3. Creating redaction overlay...\")\n        writer = self.create_redaction_overlay(input_path, rects)\n\n        # Step 4: Clear metadata\n        print(\"4. Clearing sensitive metadata...\")\n        self.clear_metadata(writer)\n\n        # Step 5: Save anonymized PDF\n        print(f\"5. Saving anonymized PDF to: {output_path}\")\n        output_path.parent.mkdir(parents=True, exist_ok=True)\n        with open(output_path, 'wb') as f:\n            writer.write(f)\n\n        print(f\"✓ Successfully anonymized: {output_path}\")\n        return True\n\n\ndef main():\n    \"\"\"Process all papers.\"\"\"\n    input_dir = Path(\"/root\")\n    output_dir = Path(\"/root/redacted\")\n\n    papers = [\n        input_dir / \"paper1.pdf\",\n        input_dir / \"paper2.pdf\",\n        input_dir / \"paper3.pdf\",\n    ]\n\n    anonymizer = PDFAnonymizer()\n\n    for paper_path in papers:\n        if paper_path.exists():\n            output_path = output_dir / paper_path.name\n            try:\n                anonymizer.anonymize_pdf(paper_path, output_path)\n            except Exception as e:\n                print(f\"✗ Error processing {paper_path}: {e}\")\n                import traceback\n                traceback.print_exc()\n        else:\n            print(f\"✗ File not found: {paper_path}\")\n\n    print(\"\\n\" + \"=\" * 60)\n    print(\"Anonymization complete!\")\n    print(f\"Redacted PDFs saved to: {output_dir}\")\n\n\nif __name__ == \"__main__\":\n    main()\n"}}],"stop_reason":null,"stop_sequence":null,"usage":{"input_tokens":4,"cache_creation_input_tokens":142,"cache_read_input_tokens":23986,"cache_creation":{"ephemeral_5m_input_tokens":142,"ephemeral_1h_input_tokens":0},"output_tokens":1},"context_management":null},"parent_tool_use_id":null,"session_id":"4d4cb9a5-eb57-4c9d-870e-459a68c3f925","uuid":"4e3986dc-1e2a-492e-b75c-538309b54368"}

+1 for space\n\n                    if match_words:\n                        # Get bounding box for all matched words\n                        x0 = min(w['x0'] for w in match_words)\n                        x1 = max(w['x1'] for w in match_words)\n                        top = min(w['top'] for w in match_words)\n                        bottom = max(w['bottom'] for w in match_words)\n\n                        # Add padding\n                        padding = 1\n                        rects.append({\n                            'page': page_num,\n                            'x0': x0 - padding,\n                            'top': top - padding,\n                            'x1': x1 + padding,\n                            'bottom': bottom + padding\n                        })\n\n        return rects\n\n    def create_redaction_overlay(self, pdf_path, rects):\n        \"\"\"Create a PDF overlay with black rectangles covering sensitive content.\"\"\"\n        reader = PdfReader(pdf_path)\n        writer = PdfWriter()\n\n        # Group rectangles by page\n        rects_by_page = {}\n        for rect in rects:\n            page = rect['page']\n            if page not in rects_by_page:\n                rects_by_page[page] = []\n            rects_by_page[page].append(rect)\n\n        # Process each page\n        for page_num, page_obj in enumerate(reader.pages):\n            writer.add_page(page_obj)\n\n            # Create overlay for this page if needed\n            if page_num in rects_by_page:\n                page_rects = rects_by_page[page_num]\n\n                # Create overlay canvas\n                overlay_buffer = BytesIO()\n                overlay_canvas = canvas.Canvas(overlay_buffer, pagesize=(\n                    float(page_obj.mediabox.width),\n                    float(page_obj.mediabox.height)\n                ))\n\n                # Convert PDF coordinates (top-left origin) to reportlab (bottom-left)\n                    y0 = height - bottom\n                    y1 = height - top\n\n                    overlay_canvas.setFillColor((0, 0, 0))\n                    overlay_canvas.rect(x0, y0, x1 - x0, y1 - y0, fill=1, stroke=0)\n\n                overlay_canvas.save()\n                overlay_buffer.seek(0)\n\n                # Merge overlay with page\n                from PyPDF2 import PdfReader as PDFReader\n                overlay_pdf = PDFReader(overlay_buffer)\n                overlay_page = overlay_pdf.pages[0]\n                writer.pages[-1].merge_page(overlay_page)\n\n        return writer\n\n    def clear_metadata(self, writer):\n        \"\"\"Remove sensitive metadata from PDF.\"\"\"\n        if writer.metadata is None:\n            writer.add_metadata({})\n\n        # Clear potentially sensitive metadata fields\n        sensitive_fields = ['/Author', '/Creator', '/Producer', '/Subject', '/Keywords']\n        for field in sensitive_fields:\n            if field in writer.metadata:\n                del writer.metadata[field]\n\n        # Set minimal metadata\n        writer.add_metadata({\n            '/Producer': 'Anonymized PDF',\n        })\n\n    def anonymize_pdf(self, input_path, output_path):\n        \"\"\"Main anonymization pipeline.\"\"\"\n        print(f\"\\nProcessing: {input_path}\")\n        print(\"=\" * 60)\n\n        # Step 1: Extract text and find sensitive content\n        print(\"1. Extracting text and finding sensitive content...\")\n        text_data = self.extract_text_with_positions(input_path)\n\n        # Collect all text for pattern matching\n        full_text = \"\\n\".join([page['text'] or \"\" for page in text_data.values()])\n\n        # Find patterns\n        sensitive_patterns = {}\n        for pattern_name, pattern in self.patterns.items():\n            matches = list(re.finditer(pattern, full_text, re.IGNORECASE))\n            if matches:\n                print(f\"   Found {len(matches)} {pattern_name} identifiers\")\n                sensitive_patterns[pattern_name] = pattern\n\n        # Step 2: Find redaction rectangles\n        print(\"2. Finding text regions to redact...\")\n        rects = self.find_redaction_rects(text_data, sensitive_patterns)\n        print(f\"   Found {len(rects)} regions to redact\")\n\n        # Step 3: Create overlay and merge\n        print(\"3. Creating redaction overlay...\")\n        writer = self.create_redaction_overlay(input_path, rects)\n\n        # Step 4: Clear metadata\n        print(\"4. Finally,acrucialbaselineofFactualrecall,with1,000\n   166→SimpleQAquestions(Weietal.,2024),isolatesarchitecturaloverheadfromreasoningcomplexity.\n   167→Thecreationofthese2,931episodesinvolvedarigorouscurationprocessthatfilteredapproximately\n   168→22,000 source items to prioritize diagnostic clarity. Each adapted episode first achieved a mean\n   169→qualityscoreof9.0,asassessedbyanLLMvalidator,beforebeingrenderedto24kHzaudiousing\n   170→Higgs-Audio v2 (Boson AI, 2025). This TTS system is critical to the benchmark’s design, as it\n   171→automatically varies timbre, tone, and emotion based on textual content to produce acoustically\n   172→diversespeech. Toensurethefinalbenchmark’sintegrity,wevalidatedbothitssemanticandacoustic\n   173→properties. Amanualauditof200episodes(6.8%)confirmedthatsemanticandlogicalstructures\n   174→were preserved, while ananalysis of speakerembeddings usingWeSpeaker (Wang et al.,2023a)\n   175→verifiedtheacousticdiversityofthegeneratedaudio(µ=0.000,σ =0.120),confirmingtheabsence\n   176→ofsystemicacousticbias.\n   177→4 EXPERIMENTAL SETUP\n   178→4.1 EVALUATIONMETHODOLOGY\n   179→SpeechFidelityAssessment. WeevaluategeneratedspeechusingWordErrorRate(WER),compar-\n   180→ingASRtranscriptsagainstgroundtruth. OurLLM-basednormalizerstandardizesboththereference\n   181→textandASRtranscripttocanonicalmathematicalnotation(e.g.,“fofsixteenequalsfiftyfour”→\n   182→“f(16)=54”,“twentytwenty-four”→“2024”)beforecomparison. Thisnormalization,withfurther\n   183→examplesinAppendixB,ensuresafaircomparisonbetweenmathematicalexpressionsinwritten\n   184→formandtheirspokenequivalents(Sproat&Jaitly,2017).\n   185→AccuracyEvaluation. WeassesstaskaccuracyusinganLLM-as-a-judgeprotocol(Zhengetal.,\n   186→2023; Liu et al., 2023). This approach is highly effective for VERA because our benchmark\n   187→tasks,whilechallenging,aredesignedtohavewell-definedgroundtruthanswerswithminimal\n   188→ambiguity, making them suitable for reliable automated grading. We employ GPT-4o (OpenAI,\n   189→2024a)asthegrader,usingthenormalizedASRtranscriptforvoicemodeloutputs. {"type":"user","message":{"role":"user","content":[{"tool_use_id":"toolu_vrtx_016PAz5dqPUJqC1qWuMhQTzL","type":"tool_result","content":"SPEECHPRUNE: Context-aware Token Pruning for Speech Information Retrieval\n\nand potentially distracting dialogues, thereby assessing the\nmodel ability to pinpoint and retrieve essential content from\nlong-form inputs.\n\nPreliminary experiments on SPIRAL reveal limitations of\ncurrent models in handling SIR tasks, due to fundamental\narchitectural constraints. Regardless of how audio inputs\nare encoded, Speech LLMs concatenate the derived audio\ntokens/embeddings with text tokens for later processing.\nHowever, audio signals typically yield substantially longer\ntoken sequences than text inputs, dominating the computa-\ntional cost and leading to significant inefficiency due to the\nquadratic complexity of attention with respect to the input\nlength [9]. In fact, most existing models limit the length\nof input audio files to only 30 seconds [6] (about 1500 raw\ntokens when using Whisper [10] for speech encoding, and\nmodels typically add adapters to downscale the number of\ntokens), as otherwise the audio token sequence could easily\ncause out-of-memory error on GPU. Obviously, such a limi-\ntation is restrictive for Speech LLMs to handle long-form\naudio inputs longer than 30 seconds.\n\nTo address the limitation, our second technical contribution\nis SPEECHPRUNE, a training-free token pruning method\nthat enables off-the-shelf Speech LLMs to handle lengthy\naudio input efficiently and effectively. Unlike existing vision-\ncentric pruning methods (e.g., PruMerge [11]) that are in-\ncompatible with speech encoders, SPEECHPRUNE is specif-\nically designed to preserve the temporal nature of audio\nsignals. SPEECHPRUNE features a two-phase process (vi-\nsualized in Fig. 1), where it first removes semantically ir-\nrelevant speech tokens by examining the cosine similarity\nbetween speech and text token embeddings, and then further\nselects the most important tokens by approximating token\nimportance with binarized attention weights from the first\nlayer. This plug-and-play approach maintains semantic fi-\ndelity while substantially reducing computational overhead,\nmaking the processing of long audio inputs possible with-\nout any additional training upon pre-trained models. Our\nSPEECHPRUNE, which, to the best of our knowledge is\nthe first token pruning method for Speech LLMs, achieves\nnearly 29% (and 47%) higher accuracy than the original\nmodel (and the random pruning baseline) at a 20% pruning\nrate and sustains performance even when pruning 80% of\nthe input on our SPIRAL benchmark.\n\n1.1. Task Formulation\n\nWe propose the SIR task to evaluate the ability of Speech\nLLMs to identify and extract critical information from ex-\ntended spoken dialogues. This task addresses the practical\nchallenge of finding key details within lengthy conversa-\ntions, akin to finding a “needle in a haystack,” which is\nparticularly challenging given most models’ constraint of\nprocessing only 30-second audio segments.\n\nThe task is formulated as follows.\nInputs include (1) a\nlong-form speech input A = {a1, a2, . . . , an} comprising\nsequential audio segments ai, where each ai represents\na continuous segment of the spoken dialogue, and (2) a\ntextual query q that targets a specific piece of information\nmentioned or discussed at some unknown time within the\nspeech. The model must process the entire sequence A to\nlocate the relevant information that answers the query q.\nThis can be formally expressed as:\n\nr∗ = f (A, q),\n\n(1)\n\nwhere r∗ stands for the correct response, f represents the\nmodel’s function of processing speech, identifying salient\ninformation, and reasoning about the query. The critical\ninformation is contained within some segment al at position\nl, but this location is not provided to the model explicitly.\n\nTo ensure accurate evaluation without ambiguity, we struc-\nture all queries as multiple-choice questions, following es-\ntablished practice [6–8]. For each query q, the model selects\nfrom four possible responses R = {r1, r2, r3, r4}.\n\n1.2. Benchmark Construction\n\nWe introduce SPIRAL (Speech Information Retrieval And\nLookup), a novel benchmark designed to evaluate Speech\nLLMs’ ability to process long and realistic spoken inputs.\nThe samples feature three representative scenarios: lectures,\nmeetings, and daily conversations.\n\nTranscript Generation: This stage employs GPT-4o to\nsimulate rich, multi-turn dialogues. The process focuses on\nincorporating natural speech elements (e.g., fillers like “uh”\nand “oh,” hesitations) to enhance authenticity and capture\nrealistic conversational dynamics with variable turn lengths.\nFor evaluation, multiple-choice questions targeting specific\ninformation within these dialogues are also generated.\n\nSpeech Sample Synthesis: We utilize StyleTTS 2 [12],\na zero-shot TTS engine trained on LibriTTS [13]. Speak-\ners are randomly selected with balanced gender representa-\ntion. Dialogue turns are concatenated to create continuous\nspeech.\n\nThe SPIRAL dataset is open-source. We also propose\nSPIRAL-H, a challenging subset of 401 cases where the\noriginal Qwen-2 Audio model (used in our experiments)\nachieves 0% accuracy, specifically designed to test model\nrobustness on difficult instances.\n\n1.3. Quality Assessment\n\nThe SPIRAL dataset contains 1,012 samples, with an av-\nerage duration of 87.89 seconds. Whisper-v3-large [10]\nachieves a word error rate of 0.0389 on the samples.\nUTMOS-22 [14] yields a predicted Mean Opinion Score\n(MOS) of 3.91 (5-point scale).\n\n2\n\n\fSPEECHPRUNE: Context-aware Token Pruning for Speech Information Retrieval\n\nFigure 1: The proposed SPEECHPRUNE, with two phases of token pruning.\n\n1.4. Preliminaries\n\neach frame i, mean similarity ˆFi is:\n\nAudio Encoder: Speech LLMs (e.g., using Whisper [10])\ntransform raw audio into lower-dimensional embeddings. A\n30-second audio yields approximately 750 encoding embed-\ndings from Whisper, where each frame output corresponds\nto about 40ms of the original audio.\n\nLanguage Modeling: Audio tokens are projected (e.g.,\nby MLP [15] or Q-Former [16]) and concatenated with\ntext tokens for the LLM backbone [17]. Self-attention is\ncomputed as:\n\n(cid:88)\n\nˆFi =\n\nj = 0f −1 mean(Fi·f +j,:, axis = 1).\n\n(4)\n\nToken retention p = softmax( ˆF), expected tokens per\nframe ni = ⌊N pi⌋. SPEECHPRUNE Methodology\n\nWe propose a two-phase token pruning approach (Fig. 1).\n\nFirst Phase Pruning by Token-Text Similarity: We pro-\ncess input to get speech embedding S ∈ RN ×D and text\nembedding T ∈ RL×D. Token-level similarity F ∈ RN ×L\nis:\n\n(Wb\n\nQ, Wb\n\nK, Sb) = sign(WQ, WK, Sp1).\n\nApproximate attention scores:\n\nQ′ = SbWb\n\nA = softmax\n\nK,\n\n(cid:32)\n\nQ, K′ = SbWb\n(cid:33)\nQ′K′⊤\n√\ndk\n\n.\n\n(6)\n\n(7)\n\n(8)\n\nFinal token selection: Sp2 = Sp1[topk(mean(A, axis =\n1), k)]. This phase is highly efficient.\n\nF =\n\nS\n∥S∥2\n\n·\n\nT⊤\n∥T∥2\n\n.\n\n(3)\n\n1.6. Main Experiments\n\nAn adaptive frame-level approach evaluates speech seg-\nments as one-second frames (m = ⌈N/f ⌉ frames).
